content="2026/07/28" /><meta name="DC.Identifier" content="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/sites/books/NBK1488/" /><meta name="description" content="PTEN hamartoma tumor syndrome (PHTS) is characterized by skin and mucosal lesions, macrocephaly, gastrointestinal polyps, and a high risk for benign and malignant tumors of the breast, thyroid, and endometrium. There is also an increased risk of renal cell carcinoma, colorectal carcinoma, and melanoma. Some individuals have developmental delay and/or autism spectrum disorder. PHTS includes the following clinical phenotypes: Cowden syndrome (CS), Bannayan-Riley-Ruvalcaba syndrome (BRRS), Proteus-like syndrome, and apparently isolated adult-onset Lhermitte-Duclos disease (LDD). Regardless of clinical phenotype, individuals with a germline PTEN pathogenic variant are thought to have the same cancer risks." /><meta name="og:title" content="PTEN Hamartoma Tumor Syndrome" /><meta name="og:type" content="book" /><meta name="og:description" content="PTEN hamartoma tumor syndrome (PHTS) is characterized by skin and mucosal lesions, macrocephaly, gastrointestinal polyps, and a high risk for benign and malignant tumors of the breast, thyroid, and endometrium. There is also an increased risk of renal cell carcinoma, colorectal carcinoma, and melanoma. Some individuals have developmental delay and/or autism spectrum disorder. PHTS includes the following clinical phenotypes: Cowden syndrome (CS), Bannayan-Riley-Ruvalcaba syndrome (BRRS), Proteus-like syndrome, and apparently isolated adult-onset Lhermitte-Duclos disease (LDD). Regardless of clinical phenotype, individuals with a germline PTEN pathogenic variant are thought to have the same cancer risks." /><meta name="og:url"             <div class="main-content lit-style" itemscope="itemscope" itemtype="http://schema.org/CreativeWork"><div class="meta-content fm-sec"><h1 id="_NBK1488_"><span class="title" itemprop="name"><i>PTEN</i> Hamartoma Tumor Syndrome</span></h1><div itemprop="alternativeHeadline" class="subtitle whole_rhythm">Synonym: PHTS</div><p class="contrib-group"><span itemprop="author">Timothy Tramontana</span>, MD, MS and <span itemprop="author">Lamis Yehia</span>, PhD.</p><a data-jig="ncbitoggler" href="#__NBK1488_ai__" style="border:0;text-decoration:none">Author Information and Affiliations</a><div style="display:none" class="ui-widget" id="__NBK1488_ai__"><div class="contrib half_rhythm"><span itemprop="author">Timothy Tramontana</span>, MD, MS<div class="affiliation small">PTEN Multidisciplinary Clinic and Center of Excellence;<br />Center for Personalized Genetic Healthcare<br />Community Care<br />Cleveland Clinic<br />Cleveland, Ohio<div><span class="email-label">Email: </span><a href="mailto:dev@null" data-email="gro.fcc@tnomart" class="oemail">gro.fcc@tnomart</a></div></div></div><div class="contrib half_rhythm"><span itemprop="author">Lamis Yehia</span>, PhD<div class="affiliation small">Epilepsy Center<br />Neurological Institute<br />Cleveland Clinic<br />Cleveland, Ohio<div><span class="email-label">Email: </span><a href="mailto:dev@null" data-email="gro.fcc@laihey" class="oemail">gro.fcc@laihey</a></div></div></div></div><p class="small">Initial Posting: <span itemprop="datePublished">November 29, 2001</span>; Last Update: <span itemprop="dateModified">July 28, 2026</span>.</p><p><em>Estimated reading time: 43 minutes</em></p></div><div class="jig-ncbiinpagenav body-content whole_rhythm" data-jigconfig="allHeadingLevels: ['h2'],smoothScroll: false" itemprop="text"><div id="phts.Summary" itemprop="description"><h2 id="_phts_Summary_">Summary</h2><div><h4 class="inline">Clinical characteristics.</h4><p><i>PTEN</i> hamartoma tumor syndrome (PHTS) is characterized by skin and mucosal lesions, macrocephaly, gastrointestinal polyps, and a high risk for benign and malignant tumors of the breast, thyroid, and endometrium. There is also an increased risk of renal cell carcinoma, colorectal carcinoma, and melanoma. Some individuals have developmental delay and/or autism spectrum disorder. PHTS includes the following clinical phenotypes: Cowden syndrome (CS), Bannayan-Riley-Ruvalcaba syndrome (BRRS), Proteus-like syndrome, and apparently <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/isolated/">isolated</a> adult-onset Lhermitte-Duclos disease (LDD). Regardless of clinical <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a>, individuals with a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> <i>PTEN</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> are thought to have the same cancer risks.</p></div><div><h4 class="inline">Diagnosis/testing.</h4><p>The diagnosis of PHTS is established in a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> with a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> <i>PTEN</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> identified by <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/molecular-genetic-testing/">molecular genetic testing</a>. </p></div><div><h4 class="inline">Management.</h4><p><i>Treatment of manifestations:</i> Management per multidisciplinary care team, ideally with PHTS expertise, includes treatment for symptomatic skin lesions; endoscopy with polypectomy; standard treatment for colorectal cancer, renal cell carcinoma, and endometrial cancer; monitoring of thyroid nodules and multinodular goiter; treatment of breast cancer per breast cancer specialist; prophylactic mastectomy as needed; preventative hysterectomy on completion of childbearing; total abdominal hysterectomy for multiple fibroids; developmental and educational support; seizure treatment per neurologist; management of vascular anomalies per multidisciplinary vascular team; for atypical LDD, referral to neuro-oncologist, neurologist, and neurosurgeon; for those with suspected cerebrospinal fluid obstruction, referral for surgical evaluation; treatment of musculoskeletal features per orthopedist and physical therapist; family and social work support.</p><p><i>Surveillance:</i> Annual comprehensive physical and dermatologic exam; education regarding signs and symptoms of cancer annually starting at age 18 years or five years before the youngest cancer diagnosis in the family; annual thyroid exam and ultrasound starting at age 12 years; monthly breast self-exam starting at age 18 years; clinical breast exam every six to 12 months starting at age 25 years; mammogram with consideration of tomosynthesis and breast MRI with contrast annually starting at age 30 years; consider kidney imaging every one to two years starting at age 40 years; assess for symptoms of endometrial cancer every one to two years starting at age 30 years with consideration of endometrial biopsy; transvaginal ultrasound in postmenopausal women as needed; assess for clinical manifestations of gastrointestinal polyps and colorectal carcinoma annually; colonoscopy every five years starting at age 35 years and more frequently in those with polyps; monitor developmental progress and educational needs annually throughout childhood and adolescence; assessment for autism spectrum disorder as needed; brain imaging every three to 12 months in those with typical LDD; brain imaging every six to 12 months following LDD resection; monitor meningiomas with imaging; annual clinical assessment for scoliosis and musculoskeletal asymmetry; in those with clinical <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a> of Proteus syndrome, follow recommended surveillance; assess family and social work needs annually. </p><p><i>Agents/circumstances to avoid:</i> Cutaneous lesions should be excised only if malignancy is suspected or symptoms (e.g., pain, deformity, increased scarring) are significant. Surgical removal may lead to keloid formation and rapid recurrence of lesions. Therefore, a multidisciplinary team should evaluate the risks and benefits of lesion removal. Exercise caution with diagnostic/therapeutic radiation exposure; balance benefit versus potential secondary malignancy risk.</p><p><i>Evaluation of relatives at risk:</i> Molecular genetic testing for the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/familial/">familial</a> <i>PTEN</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> is recommended for at-risk relatives of an affected individual. Family members who have the <i>PTEN</i> pathogenic variant (and therefore have PHTS) require an initial evaluation and ongoing surveillance. Molecular genetic testing for the familial <i>PTEN</i> pathogenic variant in early childhood is often appropriate because timely detection of early-onset manifestations can guide management. </p></div><div><h4 class="inline">Genetic counseling.</h4><p>PHTS is inherited in an <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-dominant/">autosomal dominant</a> manner. Some individuals diagnosed with PHTS have an affected parent. (Note: Because external manifestations of PHTS may be subtle or variably expressed, diagnoses within families are sometimes only recognized after identification of a more severely affected individual.) Approximately 10%-44% of individuals diagnosed with PHTS have the disorder as the result of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/de-novo/"><i>de novo</i></a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>. Each child of an individual with PHTS has a 50% chance of inheriting the <i>PTEN</i> pathogenic variant. Once the <i>PTEN</i> pathogenic variant has been identified in an affected family member, predictive genetic testing for at-risk family members and prenatal/<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/preimplantation-genetic-testing/">preimplantation genetic testing</a> are possible.</p></div></div><div id="phts.GeneReview_Scope"><h2 id="_phts_GeneReview_Scope_"><i>GeneReview</i> Scope</h2><div id="phts.Tc" class="table"><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/table/phts.Tc/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__phts.Tc_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_phts.Tc_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><i>PTEN</i> Hamartoma Tumor Syndrome: Included Phenotypes&#x000a0;<sup>1</sup></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.Tc_1_1_1_1" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div><i>PTEN</i>-related Cowden syndrome</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div><i>PTEN</i>-related Bannayan-Riley-Ruvalcaba syndrome </div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div><i>PTEN</i>-related Proteus-like syndrome</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div><i>PTEN</i>-related apparently <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/isolated/">isolated</a> adult-onset Lhermitte-Duclos disease</div></li></ul>
</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">For synonyms and outdated names see <a href="#phts.Nomenclature">Nomenclature</a>.</p></div></dd><dt>1. </dt><dd><div id="phts.TF.c.1"><p class="no_margin">For other genetic causes of these phenotypes see <a href="#phts.Differential_Diagnosis">Differential Diagnosis</a>.</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div><div id="phts.Diagnosis"><h2 id="_phts_Diagnosis_">Diagnosis</h2><p>Clinical diagnostic criteria cannot be used to establish the diagnosis of <i>PTEN</i> hamartoma tumor syndrome (PHTS); molecular testing is required to establish the diagnosis. A <a href="https://www.lerner.ccf.org/genomic-medicine/ccscore/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">risk assessment tool</a> is available to determine the likelihood of identifying a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> <i>PTEN</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> in a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a>. </p><div id="phts.Suggestive_Findings"><h3>Suggestive Findings</h3><p>PHTS <b>should be suspected</b> in a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> with the following clinical and imaging features and family history.</p><p>
<b>Clinical features in children</b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Macrocephaly</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Autism spectrum disorder</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Developmental delay&#x000a0;/ intellectual disability</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Skin and mucosal lesions: lipomas, trichilemmomas (see <a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK1488/figure/phts.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figphtsF1" rid-ob="figobphtsF1">Figure 1</a>), oral mucosal papillomatosis, papillomatous papules (see <a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK1488/figure/phts.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figphtsF2" rid-ob="figobphtsF2">Figure 2</a>), glycogenic acanthosis, acral (palmoplantar) keratoses, fibromas, penile freckling</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Hamartomatous intestinal polyps (Other histologic types observed include ganglioneuromas, juvenile polyps, hyperplastic polyps, and adenomatous polyps.)</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Thyroid lesions, disease, and/or cancer: multinodular goiter, adenomatous nodules, follicular adenomas, Hashimoto thyroiditis, thyroid cancer (epithelial [non-medullary], especially follicular)</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Vascular malformations: arteriovenous malformations, hemangiomas</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Germ cell tumors (ovarian or testicular)</div></li></ul><div class="iconblock whole_rhythm clearfix ten_col fig" id="figphtsF1" co-legend-rid="figlgndphtsF1"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/figure/phts.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" title="Figure 1. " class="img_link icnblk_img figpopup" rid-figpopup="figphtsF1" rid-ob="figobphtsF1"><img class="small-thumb" src="/sites/books/NBK1488/bin/phts-Image001.gif" src-large="/sites/books/NBK1488/bin/phts-Image001.jpg" alt="Figure 1. " /></a><div class="icnblk_cntnt" id="figlgndphtsF1"><h4 id="phts.F1"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/figure/phts.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobphtsF1">Figure 1. </a></h4><p class="float-caption no_bottom_margin">Trichilemmoma </p></div></div><div class="iconblock whole_rhythm clearfix ten_col fig" id="figphtsF2" co-legend-rid="figlgndphtsF2"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/figure/phts.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" title="Figure 2. " class="img_link icnblk_img figpopup" rid-figpopup="figphtsF2" rid-ob="figobphtsF2"><img class="small-thumb" src="/sites/books/NBK1488/bin/phts-Image002.gif" src-large="/sites/books/NBK1488/bin/phts-Image002.jpg" alt="Figure 2. " /></a><div class="icnblk_cntnt" id="figlgndphtsF2"><h4 id="phts.F2"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/figure/phts.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobphtsF2">Figure 2. </a></h4><p class="float-caption no_bottom_margin">Papillomatous papules in the periocular region (A) and on the dorsum of the hand (B) </p></div></div><p>
<b>Additional clinical and imaging features in adults</b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Cancers: breast cancer, thyroid cancer (epithelial [non-medullary], especially follicular), renal cell carcinoma, endometrial carcinoma, colorectal carcinoma, and melanoma</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Fibrocystic disease of the breast</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Uterine fibroids</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Genitourinary malformations (e.g., bicornuate uterus, duplicated ureters)</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Adult-onset Lhermitte-Duclos disease, defined as the presence of a cerebellar dysplastic gangliocytoma with a characteristic "tiger-striped" appearance</div></li></ul><p><b>Family history</b> is consistent with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-dominant/">autosomal dominant</a> inheritance (e.g., affected males and females in multiple generations). Absence of a known family history does not preclude the diagnosis.</p></div><div id="phts.Establishing_the_Diagnosis"><h3>Establishing the Diagnosis</h3><p>The diagnosis of PHTS <b>is established</b> in a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> with <a href="#phts.Suggestive_Findings">suggestive findings</a> and a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> pathogenic (or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-pathogenic/">likely pathogenic</a>) variant in <i>PTEN</i> identified by <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/molecular-genetic-testing/">molecular genetic testing</a> (see <a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/table/phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobphtsTptenhamartomatumorsyndromemol">Table 1</a>). </p><p>Note: (1) Per American College of Medical Genetics and Genomics (ACMG)&#x000a0;/ Association for Molecular Pathology variant interpretation guidelines, the terms "<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>" and "<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-pathogenic/">likely pathogenic</a> variant" are synonymous in a clinical setting, meaning that both are considered diagnostic and can be used for clinical decision making [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.richards.2015.405">Richards et al 2015</a>]. Reference to "pathogenic variants" in this <i>GeneReview</i> is understood to include likely pathogenic variants. (2) Identification of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> <i>PTEN</i> variant of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/uncertain-significance/">uncertain significance</a> does not establish or rule out the diagnosis. </p><p>Molecular genetic testing approaches can include a combination of <b><a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>-targeted testing</b> (single-gene testing, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/multigene-panel/">multigene panel</a>) and <b>comprehensive</b>
<b><a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genomic/">genomic</a> testing</b> (<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exome-sequencing/">exome sequencing</a>, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genome-sequencing/">genome sequencing</a>). Gene-targeted testing requires that the clinician determine which gene(s) are likely involved (see <a href="#phts.Option_1">Option 1</a>), whereas comprehensive genomic testing does not (see <a href="#phts.Option_2">Option 2</a>).</p><div id="phts.Option_1"><h4>Option 1</h4><p><b>Single-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> testing.</b> Sequence analysis of <i>PTEN</i> is performed first to detect <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/missense/">missense</a>, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/nonsense-variant/">nonsense</a>, and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/splice-site/">splice site</a> variants and small intragenic deletions/insertions. Note: Depending on the sequencing method used, single-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exon/">exon</a>, multiexon, or whole-gene deletions/duplications may not be detected. If no variant is detected by the sequencing method used, the next step is to perform gene-targeted <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/deletion-duplication-analysis/">deletion/duplication analysis</a> to detect exon and whole-gene deletions or duplications. If a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> is not identified with deletion/duplication analysis, perform <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/sequence-analysis/">sequence analysis</a> of the <i>PTEN</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/promoter-region/">promoter region</a> for variants that decrease <i>PTEN</i> gene expression.</p><p><b>A <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/multigene-panel/">multigene panel</a></b> that includes <i>PTEN</i> and other genes of interest (<i>AKT1</i>, <i>PIK3CA</i>, <i>SDHB</i>, <i>SDHC</i>, <i>SDHD</i>, <i>SEC23B</i>, and <i>WWP1</i>; see <a href="#phts.Differential_Diagnosis">Differential Diagnosis</a>) may also be considered to identify the genetic cause of the condition while limiting identification of pathogenic variants and variants of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/uncertain-significance/">uncertain significance</a> in genes that do not explain the underlying <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a>. Note: (1) The genes included in the panel and the diagnostic <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/sensitivity/">sensitivity</a> of the testing used for each <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> vary by laboratory and are likely to change over time. (2) Some multigene panels may include genes not associated with the condition discussed in this <i>GeneReview</i>. (3) In some laboratories, panel options may include a custom laboratory-designed panel and/or custom phenotype-focused <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exome/">exome</a> analysis that includes genes specified by the clinician. (4) Methods used in a panel may include <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/sequence-analysis/">sequence analysis</a>, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/deletion-duplication-analysis/">deletion/duplication analysis</a>, and/or other non-sequencing-based tests. </p><p>For an introduction to multigene panels click <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app5/#app5.Multigene_Panels">here</a>. More detailed information for clinicians ordering genetic tests can be found <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app5/#app5.Multigene_Panels_FAQs">here</a>.</p></div><div id="phts.Option_2"><h4>Option 2</h4><p><b>Comprehensive</b>
<b><a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genomic/">genomic</a> testing</b> does not require the clinician to determine which <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> is likely involved. <b>Exome sequencing</b> is most commonly used; <b><a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genome-sequencing/">genome sequencing</a></b> is also possible. ACMG and the American Academy of Pediatrics recommend <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exome/">exome</a>/genome sequencing as first- or second-tier diagnostic testing for children with developmental delay, intellectual disability, and/or multiple <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/congenital/">congenital</a> anomalies [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.manickam.2021.2029">Manickam et al 2021</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.rodan.2025.e2025072219">Rodan et al 2025</a>]. </p><p>For an introduction to comprehensive <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genomic/">genomic</a> testing click <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app5/#app5.Comprehensive_Genomic_Testing">here</a>. More detailed information for clinicians ordering genomic testing can be found <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app5/#app5.Comprehensive_Genomic_Testing_1">here</a>.</p><div id="phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 1. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p><i>PTEN</i> Hamartoma Tumor Syndrome: Molecular Genetic Testing</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/table/phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Gene&#x000a0;<sup>1</sup></th><th id="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Method</th><th id="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol_1_1_1_3" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Proportion of Pathogenic Variants&#x000a0;<sup>2</sup> Identified by Method</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol_1_1_1_1" rowspan="3" scope="row" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>PTEN</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Sequence analysis of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/coding-region/">coding region</a>&#x000a0;<sup>3</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">&#x02264;80%&#x000a0;<sup>4</sup></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol_1_1_1_2" colspan="1" scope="row" rowspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Gene-targeted <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/deletion-duplication-analysis/">deletion/duplication analysis</a>&#x000a0;<sup>5</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">3%-11%&#x000a0;<sup>4</sup></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol_1_1_1_2" colspan="1" scope="row" rowspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Sequence analysis of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/promoter-region/">promoter region</a></td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_mol_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">~10%&#x000a0;<sup>4</sup></td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt>1. </dt><dd><div id="phts.TF.1.1"><p class="no_margin">See <a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/#phts.molgen.TA">Table A. Genes and Databases</a> for <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/chromosome/">chromosome</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/locus/">locus</a> and protein.</p></div></dd><dt>2. </dt><dd><div id="phts.TF.1.2"><p class="no_margin">See <a href="#phts.Molecular_Genetics">Molecular Genetics</a> for information on variants detected in this <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>. </p></div></dd><dt>3. </dt><dd><div id="phts.TF.1.3"><p class="no_margin">Sequence analysis detects variants that are benign, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-benign/">likely benign</a>, of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/uncertain-significance/">uncertain significance</a>, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-pathogenic/">likely pathogenic</a>, or pathogenic. Variants may include <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/missense/">missense</a>, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/nonsense-variant/">nonsense</a>, and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/splice-site/">splice site</a> variants and small intragenic deletions/insertions; typically, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exon/">exon</a> or whole-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> deletions/duplications are not detected. For issues to consider in interpretation of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/sequence-analysis/">sequence analysis</a> results, click <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app2/">here</a>.</p></div></dd><dt>4. </dt><dd><div id="phts.TF.1.4"><p class="no_margin"><a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2019.452">Yehia et al [2019]</a> and data derived from the subscription-based professional view of Human Gene Mutation Database [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.stenson.2020.1197">Stenson et al 2020</a>]</p></div></dd><dt>5. </dt><dd><div id="phts.TF.1.5"><p class="no_margin">Gene-targeted <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/deletion-duplication-analysis/">deletion/duplication analysis</a> detects intragenic deletions or duplications. Methods used may include a range of techniques such as <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/quantitative-pcr/">quantitative PCR</a>, long-range PCR, multiplex ligation-dependent probe amplification (MLPA), and a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>-targeted microarray designed to detect single-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exon/">exon</a> deletions or duplications. Exome and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genome-sequencing/">genome sequencing</a> may be able to detect deletions/duplications using breakpoint detection or read depth; however, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/sensitivity/">sensitivity</a> can be lower than gene-targeted deletion/duplication analysis.</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div></div></div><div id="phts.Clinical_Characteristics"><h2 id="_phts_Clinical_Characteristics_">Clinical Characteristics</h2><div id="phts.Clinical_Description"><h3>Clinical Description</h3><p>Individuals with <i>PTEN</i> hamartoma tumor syndrome (PHTS) diagnosed in childhood are more likely to present with macrocephaly, developmental delay, and neurobehavioral manifestations. Adults most often present with benign and malignant tumors of the breast, thyroid, and endometrium and/or multiple gastrointestinal (GI) polyps. Additional common features include skin or mucosal lesions. Some individuals have seizures, vascular anomalies, soft tissue overgrowth, and/or other cancers (renal cell carcinoma, colorectal carcinoma, and melanoma). PHTS includes the following historical clinical phenotypes: Cowden syndrome, Bannayan-Riley-Ruvalcaba syndrome, Proteus-like syndrome, and apparently <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/isolated/">isolated</a> adult-onset Lhermitte-Duclos disease [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2020.103">Yehia et al 2020</a>] (see <a href="#phts.Nomenclature">Nomenclature</a>).</p><div id="phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_fre" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 2. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p><i>PTEN</i> Hamartoma Tumor Syndrome: Frequency of Select Features</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/table/phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_fre/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_fre_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_fre_1_1_1_1" colspan="2" scope="colgroup" rowspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">17%</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_fre_1_1_1_1" colspan="1" scope="row" rowspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Cutaneous melanoma</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_fre_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">5%</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">Based on <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2023.e239705">Yehia et al [2023]</a></p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div><div id="phts.Childhood_Presentation_of_PHTS"><h4>Childhood Presentation of PHTS</h4><p>Children often present with macrocephaly, developmental delay, and neurobehavioral manifestations including autism spectrum disorder (ASD), though these features are not universally present [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.butler.2005.318">Butler et al 2005</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.hansenkiss.2017.471">Hansen-Kiss et al 2017</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2020.103">Yehia et al 2020</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.dhawan.2024.1301">Dhawan et al 2024</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.macfarland.2024.e63608">MacFarland et al 2024</a>].</p><p><b>Macrocephaly</b> (due to megalencephaly) is nearly universal and often severe. Head circumference is often more than five standard deviations above the mean. Macrocephaly in those with <i>PTEN</i>-related ASD is more pronounced compared to macrocephaly associated with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/idiopathic/">idiopathic</a> ASD.</p><p><b>Developmental delay (DD) and neurobehavioral/psychiatric disorders.</b> Approximately half of children diagnosed with PHTS have a neurodevelopmental disorder that includes ASD and/or DD. Children with PHTS<i>-</i>related ASD have a distinctive neurocognitive and behavioral <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a> including delayed language development, poor working memory and processing speed, and adaptive and sensory abnormalities [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.frazier.2015.1132">Frazier et al 2015</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.busch.2019.253">Busch et al 2019</a>]. Individuals with PHTS exhibit a wide range of neurocognitive outcomes, from high functioning to significant impairment. In a large study, neurodevelopmental disorders, including ASD, DD, and/or intellectual disability, were identified in 35% of children and 23% of adults with PHTS [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.dhawan.2025b.e200299">Dhawan et al 2025b</a>]. The neurocognitive phenotype is variable, with overall severity ranging from mild to severe; however, among individuals with ASD, clinical manifestations are most often in the mild-to-moderate range, although broader interindividual variability is well recognized. Psychiatric disorders, including anxiety, depression, bipolar disorder, attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, obsessive-compulsive disorder, and psychosis, have been documented in PHTS, affecting approximately 11%-44% of individuals [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.hansenkiss.2017.471">Hansen-Kiss et al 2017</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.balci.2018.101">Balci et al 2018</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.dhawan.2025b.e200299">Dhawan et al 2025b</a>]. </p><p><b>Seizures.</b> Individuals with PHTS are at increased risk for developing epilepsy. Seizures may arise in association with structural brain abnormalities, including focal cortical dysplasia, polymicrogyria, and other intracranial lesions such as cavernomas. Recent studies have estimated the prevalence of epilepsy in PHTS to range from 6% to 17%, substantially higher than the approximately 1% prevalence observed in the general population [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.liu.2024.250">Liu et al 2024</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.dhawan.2025b.e200299">Dhawan et al 2025b</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.jonker.2026.36">Jonker &#x00026; Schieving 2026</a>]. Individuals with PHTS and ASD have a higher risk of having epilepsy. Epilepsy in individuals with PHTS may be either generalized or focal [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.dhawan.2024.1301">Dhawan et al 2024</a>].</p><p><b>Dermatologic features.</b> Pigmentary features including caf&#x000e9; au lait macules and penile freckling (in males) can be seen in childhood. Children can also have one or more lipomas at presentation. Linear nevi may be present in those with overgrowth.</p><p><b>Early-onset thyroid disease and thyroid cancer.</b> Thyroid nodules are common. Hashimoto thyroiditis can occur. Early-onset thyroid cancer as young as age four years has been reported. Thyroid cancer is more often follicular than papillary. </p><p><b>GI manifestations.</b> A pediatric series reported the prevalence of GI polyps is approximately 25%-30% of individuals with PHTS [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.liu.2024.250">Liu et al 2024</a>]. The age at identification in childhood is variable and often driven by symptoms (e.g., bleeding, anemia, abdominal pain), with identification of polyps typically occurring in mid-childhood through adolescence. However, the overall range of onset spans from infancy to adolescence. Although uncommon, very early onset in infancy has been reported. These individuals usually have juvenile polyposis of infancy (JPI), associated with 10q23 microdeletions that encompass <i>PTEN</i> and <i>BMPR1A</i>. ASD-associated GI symptoms (e.g., abdominal pain, constipation, diarrhea) may be exacerbated by the presence of polyps. GI hamartomatous polyps may occasionally be associated with intussusception, but rectal bleeding and oozing of "serum" is more common. </p><p><b>Vascular anomalies and soft tissue overgrowth</b> [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.tan.2007.594">Tan et al 2007</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.kurek.2012.671">Kurek et al 2012</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.pilarski.2019.844">Pilarski 2019</a>] include hemangiomas and arteriovenous malformations (AVM), which may be extracranial (e.g., intramuscular) or intracranial (e.g., developmental venous anomalies)<i>.</i> AVM can be chronic and significantly impact quality of life. Cavernous hemangiomas are reported in some individuals.</p><p><i>PTEN</i> hamartoma of the soft tissue (PHOST) is a rare benign overgrowth involving vessels, fat, connective tissue, and nerves, leading to localized deformities that resemble Proteus syndrome.</p><p><b>Other malignancies.</b> Germ cell tumors of the testes (e.g., seminoma, testicular mixed germ-cell tumor) and ovaries (e.g., dysgerminoma, immature teratoma) have been reported rarely in children with PHTS [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2025.61">Yehia et al 2025</a>]. Case studies and case series have also identified hematologic malignancies (e.g., lymphoma), angiosarcoma, and neuroblastoma rarely in children with PHTS [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2025.61">Yehia et al 2025</a>]. </p></div><div id="phts.Adult_Presentations_of_PHTS"><h4>Adult Presentations of PHTS</h4><p>Adults with PHTS often come to medical attention after being diagnosed with cancer or multiple polyps on endoscopy. Other clinical characteristics of PHTS that are often recognized in adults include dermatologic manifestations, vascular anomalies, soft tissue overgrowth, autoimmunity, lymphoid hyperplasia, metabolic features, neurocognitive manifestations, and neuroimaging findings (including Lhermitte-Duclos disease [LDD])<i>.</i> In some individuals, LDD is the only apparent clinical manifestation. </p><p>PHTS is associated with increased lifetime risk of breast, thyroid, endometrial, kidney, colon, and prostate cancer, soft tissue sarcomas, and melanoma [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.tan.2012.400">Tan et al 2012</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.bubien.2013.255">Bubien et al 2013</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.nieuwenhuis.2014.57">Nieuwenhuis et al 2014</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.hendricks.2023.93">Hendricks et al 2023</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2023.e239705">Yehia et al 2023</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2025.61">Yehia et al 2025</a>]. PHTS is associated with increased risk of bilateral and multifocal cancers and second primary malignant neoplasms [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.ngeow.2014.1818">Ngeow et al 2014</a>]. </p><p><b>Fibrocystic breast disease&#x000a0;/ breast cancer.</b> Women with PHTS have up to 67% risk of benign breast disease. A prospective study of probands and family members with a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> <i>PTEN</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> revealed an 85%-91% lifetime risk for female breast cancer, with 50% <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/penetrance/">penetrance</a> by age 50 years [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.tan.2012.400">Tan et al 2012</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2023.e239705">Yehia et al 2023</a>]. The risk for multifocal/bilateral cancer is increased. Although breast cancer has been described in males with a <i>PTEN</i> pathogenic variant [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.fackenthal.2001.159">Fackenthal et al 2001</a>], it was not observed in a study of more than 3,000 probands [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.tan.2011.42">Tan et al 2011</a>].</p><p><b>GI polyps and colorectal cancer.</b> GI polyps are highly prevalent in individuals with PHTS, identified in more than 90% of those who have undergone upper and/or lower endoscopic evaluation [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.mcgarrity.2003.1429">McGarrity et al 2003</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.heald.2010.1927">Heald et al 2010</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.stanich.2014.1833">Stanich et al 2014</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.khare.2022.e183">Khare et al 2022</a>]. GI polyps are histologically diverse and may include hamartomatous, ganglioneuromatous, juvenile, hyperplastic, and adenomatous types. Upper GI polyps are also observed in some individuals. PHTS-related hamartomatous polyps are different in histomorphology from the polyps seen in <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/pjs/">Peutz-Jeghers syndrome</a>, with the latter characterized by a distinctive arborizing network of smooth muscle bundles extending from the muscularis mucosae and partitioning mucosal epithelium into lobules. </p><p>Approximately 20% demonstrate glycogenic acanthosis, characterized by plaques within the esophageal mucosa [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.bender.1973.373">Bender et al 1973</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.nishizawa.2009.1116">Nishizawa et al 2009</a>].</p><p>The lifetime risk of colorectal cancer in individuals with PHTS is estimated to be as high as 17%, with risk increasing beginning in the late 30s [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2023.e239705">Yehia et al 2023</a>]. Colorectal cancer in this population often arises in the setting of preexisting or concurrent colonic polyps. </p><p><b>Thyroid nodules&#x000a0;/ thyroid cancer.</b> Benign multinodular goiter of the thyroid as well as adenomatous nodules and follicular adenomas are common in adults [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.harach.1999.331">Harach et al 1999</a>]. The lifetime risk for epithelial thyroid cancer is 33%-35% [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.tan.2012.400">Tan et al 2012</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2023.e239705">Yehia et al 2023</a>]. Median age of onset is 37 years; age seven years is the youngest age at diagnosis reported to date [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.ngeow.2011.e2063">Ngeow et al 2011</a>]. Follicular histology is overrepresented in adults compared to the general population, in which papillary histology is overrepresented. No medullary thyroid carcinoma was observed in a cohort of individuals with PHTS.</p><p><b>Endometrial disease.</b> Benign uterine fibroids are common. Lifetime risk for endometrial cancer is 28%-48%, with the starting age at risk in the late 30s to early 40s [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.tan.2012.400">Tan et al 2012</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2023.e239705">Yehia et al 2023</a>].</p><p><b>Renal cell carcinoma (RCC).</b> Lifetime risk for RCC is 30%-35%, with the starting age at risk in the 20s [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.tan.2012.400">Tan et al 2012</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.kim.2020.40">Kim et al 2020</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2023.e239705">Yehia et al 2023</a>]. The predominant histology is papillary renal cell carcinoma [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.mester.2012.1187.e1">Mester et al 2012</a>], although other subtypes such as chromophobe and clear cell have been reported.</p><p><b>Prostate cancer.</b> Compared with standard population risks, a recent study observed elevated risk of prostate adenocarcinoma (standardized incidence ratio [SIR]: 4.1; 95% confidence interval [CI]: 1.9-7.8). The median age at diagnosis is 59 years (interquartile range [IQR]: 56-61) for prostate cancer versus 67 years in Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) Program of the National Cancer Institute (United States). The youngest age at diagnosis is 45 years [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2025.61">Yehia et al 2025</a>].</p><p><b>Dermatologic and mucosal manifestations.</b>
<i>C</i>ommon benign mucocutaneous lesions in adults with PHTS include primarily facial trichilemmomas, oral mucosal papillomatosis, papillomatous papules, acral/palmoplantar keratoses, and lipomas. Glycogenic acanthosis (multiple small, white, esophageal papules due to hyperplastic squamous epithelium with increased glycogen) is a characteristic mucosal feature of Cowden syndrome.</p><p>Testicular lipomatosis may occur in males [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.lindsay.2003.481">Lindsay et al 2003</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.woodhouse.2005.1151">Woodhouse et al 2005</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.woodhouse.2006.801">Woodhouse &#x00026; Ferguson 2006</a>].</p><p>Lifetime risk for cutaneous melanoma is estimated at up to 6%. The earliest age at onset is age three years [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.tan.2012.400">Tan et al 2012</a>].</p><p><b>Vascular anomalies and soft tissue overgrowth.</b> Vascular anomalies (hemangiomas, AVM) and PHOST can significantly impact quality of life due to chronic pain, progressive and infiltrative overgrowth, and functional limitations, particularly when lesions involve major muscle groups or the extremities. The clinical course is often marked by lesion growth, recurrence after intervention, and the need for repeated procedures or long&#x02011;term medical therapy. Together, these factors contribute to substantial physical morbidity, treatment burden, and psychosocial impact [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.tan.2007.594">Tan et al 2007</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.kurek.2012.671">Kurek et al 2012</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.pilarski.2019.844">Pilarski 2019</a>]<i>.</i> Cavernous hemangiomas are reported in some individuals<i>.</i></p><p><b>Soft tissue sarcomas.</b> Compared with standard population risks, a recent study observed elevated risk of soft tissue sarcomas (SIR: 10.7; 95% CI: 3.9-23.7) individuals with PHTS. The median age at diagnosis is 46 years (IQR: 28-47) compared to 62 years in the SEER Program. Diagnoses include malignant giant cell tumor of tendon sheath, chondrosarcoma, cutaneous leiomyosarcoma, Ewing sarcoma, and liposarcoma [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2025.61">Yehia et al 2025</a>].</p><p><b>Autoimmunity and lymphoid hyperplasia</b> are observed in up to approximately 40% of individuals [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.ngeow.2011.e2063">Ngeow et al 2011</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.heindl.2012.1093">Heindl et al 2012</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.henderson.2014.553">Henderson et al 2014</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.mauro.2017.9682803">Mauro et al 2017</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.liu.2024.250">Liu et al 2024</a>]<i>.</i> Reported autoimmune disorders include Hashimoto thyroiditis, eosinophilic esophagitis, colitis, and vasculitis.</p><p><b>Metabolic features</b> of PHTS in adults include increased insulin sensitivity despite higher rates of obesity; lower fasting insulin levels and higher glucose utilization; and increased body mass index (BMI) driven by greater adiposity without major changes in fat distribution<i>.</i> Adults have a paradoxical increased cancer and obesity risk but reduced risk of diabetes mellitus type 2 [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.pal.2012.1002">Pal et al 2012</a>]<i>.</i></p><p><b>Neurocognitive manifestations.</b> Adults with PHTS have a wide range of cognitive abilities, with common deficits in motor function, executive function, and memory even without any history of developmental delay in childhood [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.balci.2018.101">Balci et al 2018</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.dhamija.2018.207">Dhamija et al 2018</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.ciaccio.2019.103596">Ciaccio et al 2019</a>]<i>.</i></p><p><b>Neuroimaging findings.</b> Brain imaging may reveal white matter abnormalities, meningiomas, vascular malformations, prominent perivascular spaces, cortical dysplasia, and gray matter heterotopias. Lhermitte-Duclos disease (LDD), characterized by cerebellar hamartomatous overgrowth, is a rare but pathognomonic finding of PHTS. Imaging findings of LDD often shows unilateral cerebellar enlargement with a "tiger-striped" pattern.</p><p><b>Genitourinary malformations</b> are an infrequently reported and incompletely characterized feature of PHTS. Reported findings include bicornuate uterus (females), testicular lipomatosis (males), and duplicated ureters. The true incidence of structural genitourinary malformations in PHTS is unclear, as these features have been reported only sporadically and are not consistently observed in cohort studies.</p></div></div><div id="phts.GenotypePhenotype_Correlations"><h3>Genotype-Phenotype Correlations</h3><p>Large studies of individuals with PHTS have found that <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> <i>PTEN</i> frameshift variants are overrepresented, but not absolute, in those with thyroid cancer, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/nonsense-variant/">nonsense</a> pathogenic variants in those with colorectal cancer, promoter variants those with breast cancer, and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/missense/">missense</a> variants in individuals with autism spectrum disorder (ASD) [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.ngeow.2011.e2063">Ngeow et al 2011</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.tan.2012.400">Tan et al 2012</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.frazier.2015.1132">Frazier et al 2015</a>]. Multiple studies have demonstrated individuals with <i>PTEN</i>-related ASD had less severe pathogenic variants than those identified in individuals with PHTS without a diagnosis of ASD [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.spinelli.2015.128">Spinelli et al 2015</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.leslie.2016.30">Leslie &#x00026; Longy 2016</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.mighell.2018.943">Mighell et al 2018</a>]. </p></div><div id="phts.Penetrance"><h3>Penetrance</h3><p>Penetrance for <i>PTEN</i> pathogenic variants is age related; no non-penetrant individuals are known to be reported. </p></div><div id="phts.Nomenclature"><h3>Nomenclature</h3><p>The historical clinically defined phenotypes of Cowden syndrome, Bannayan-Riley-Ruvalcaba syndrome (BRRS), Proteus-like syndrome, and apparently <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/isolated/">isolated</a> adult-onset Lhermitte-Duclos disease (LDD) were initially thought to be disparate disorders. Cowden syndrome was originally described as an adult-onset genodermatosis that imparted increased risk for certain cancers. BRRS was considered a pediatric-onset disorder characterized by macrocephaly in combination with intestinal hamartomatous polyposis, vascular malformations, lipomas, hemangiomas, and genital freckling. Following the identification of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> <i>PTEN</i> pathogenic variants in individuals with these diagnoses, it became apparent that the four phenotypes are part of the same disease spectrum. To facilitate diagnosis and subsequent medical management, the term <i>PTEN</i> hamartoma tumor syndrome (PHTS) was coined to include all individuals with a germline <i>PTEN</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> regardless of clinical manifestations. All phenotypes associated with a <i>PTEN</i> pathogenic variant should be termed PHTS, with all of its implications for management [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2020.103">Yehia et al 2020</a>].</p><p>Cowden syndrome, Cowden disease, and multiple hamartoma syndrome have been used interchangeably.</p><p>Bannayan-Riley-Ruvalcaba syndrome, Bannayan-Ruvalcaba-Riley syndrome, Bannayan-Zonana syndrome, and Myhre-Riley-Smith syndrome refer to a similar constellation of signs that comprise what has been referred to as BRRS. </p><p>A form of <i>PTEN</i>-related Proteus-like syndrome with a clinical presentation similar to that first described by <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.zhou.2000.765">Zhou et al [2000]</a> was termed SOLAMEN (<i>s</i>egmental <i>o</i>vergrowth, <i>l</i>ipomatosis, <i>a</i>rteriovenous <i>m</i>alformation, and <i>e</i>pidermal <i>n</i>evus) syndrome [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.caux.2007.767">Caux et al 2007</a>].</p></div><div id="phts.Prevalence"><h3>Prevalence</h3><p>A recent study evaluated the prevalence of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> pathogenic or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-pathogenic/">likely pathogenic</a> <i>PTEN</i> variants in two large research cohorts [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.white.2025">White et al 2025</a>]. In the US-based All of Us Research Program, which included 245,000 studied participants, the estimated prevalence of PHTS was approximately one in 9,000. Similarly, the UK Biobank cohort, comprising 470,000 studied individuals, yielded a prevalence estimate of one in 13,000.</p></div></div><div id="phts.Genetically_Related_Allelic_Disorde"><h2 id="_phts_Genetically_Related_Allelic_Disorde_">Genetically Related (Allelic) Disorders</h2><p>No phenotypes other than those discussed in this <i>GeneReview</i> are known to be associated with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> pathogenic variants in <i>PTEN</i>. </p><p><b>Contiguous <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> deletions</b> involving <i>PTEN</i> and <i>BMPR1A</i> have been reported in individuals with juvenile polyposis of infancy (JPI) (OMIM <a href="https://omim.org/entry/612242" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">612242</a>). JPI is characterized by polyps that develop within the first few years of life, hypoproteinemia, protein-losing enteropathy, diarrhea, anemia, anasarca, and poor growth. The <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a> may mimic Bannayan-Riley-Ruvalcaba syndrome.</p><p><b>Sporadic tumors</b> (including endometrial cancer, prostate cancer, and glioblastoma) occurring as single tumors in the absence of any other findings of PHTS frequently contain a somatic variant in <i>PTEN</i> that is <b>not</b> present in the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a>. <i>PTEN</i> is one of the most frequently somatically mutated or deleted genes in cancer. In these circumstances predisposition to these tumors is not heritable. </p></div><div id="phts.Differential_Diagnosis"><h2 id="_phts_Differential_Diagnosis_">Differential Diagnosis</h2><p>Genes of interest in the differential diagnosis of <i>PTEN</i> hamartoma tumor syndrome (PHTS) are listed in <a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/table/phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobphtsTptenhamartomatumorsyndromegen">Table 3</a>.</p><div id="phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 3. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p><i>PTEN</i> Hamartoma Tumor Syndrome: Genetic Differential Diagnosis</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/table/phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Gene(s)</th><th id="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Disorder</th><th id="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_3" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">MOI</th><th id="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_4" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Clinical Characteristics</th><th id="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_5" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Comment</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>AKT1</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><i>AKT1</i>-related <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/proteus/">Proteus syndrome</a></td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Not inherited&#x000a0;<sup>1</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Progressive segmental or patchy overgrowth most commonly affecting skeleton, skin, adipose, &#x00026; CNS</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Often no manifestations at birth; develops &#x00026; progresses rapidly beginning in toddler yrs, causing severe overgrowth &#x00026; disfigurement.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Tumors, most commonly meningiomas; ovarian cystadenomas, breast cancer, parotid monomorphic adenoma, mesothelioma, &#x00026; others</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Pulmonary complications</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Striking predisposition to DVT &#x00026; pulmonary embolism</div></li></ul>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_5" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">PHTS is assoc w/growth abnormalities w/linear nevi &#x00026; vascular malformations that are clinically &#x00026; molecularly distinct from those of <i>AKT1</i>-related Proteus syndrome.</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>BMPR1A</i>
<br />
<i>SMAD4</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<a href="/sites/books/n/gene/jps/">Juvenile polyposis syndrome (JPS)</a>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AD</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Predisposition to GI hamartomatous polyps (specifically stomach, small intestine, colon, &#x00026; rectum). Untreated polyps may cause bleeding &#x00026; anemia.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Most juvenile polyps are benign, but malignant transformation can occur.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>A combined syndrome of JPS &#x00026; <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/hht/">hereditary hemorrhagic telangiectasia</a> is present in most persons w/<i>SMAD4</i> PV.</div></li></ul>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_5" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Unlike PHTS, polyps usually present by age 20 yrs &#x00026; can reach up to 100 during lifetime.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>JPS polyps are juvenile polyps by histology. PHTS polyps have diverse histology incl epithelial overgrowth, hamartomatous, neuromatous, juvenile, &#x00026; adenomatous.</div></li></ul>
</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>FLCN</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><a href="/sites/books/n/gene/bhd/">Birt-Hogg-Dub&#x000e9; syndrome</a> (BHDS)</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AD</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Cutaneous manifestations (fibrofolliculomas, acrochordons, angiofibromas, oral papules, cutaneous collagenomas, &#x00026; epidermal cysts)</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Pulmonary cysts&#x000a0;/ history of pneumothorax</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Various types of renal tumors</div></li></ul>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_5" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Cutaneous lesions can be mistaken for trichilemmomas, characteristic of PHTS.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Unlike predominant papillary renal cancers in PHTS, BHDS renal cancers have mixed chromophobe/oncocytic histology.</div></li></ul>
</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>NF1</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<a href="/sites/books/n/gene/nf1/">Neurofibromatosis 1 (NF1)</a>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AD</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">The only features seen in both NF1 &#x00026; PHTS are caf&#x000e9; au lait macules &#x00026; fibromatous tumors of the skin.</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_5" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">NF1 may be mistakenly diagnosed in persons w/PHTS due to presence of ganglioneuromas in GI tract.</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>PIK3CA</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><i>PIK3CA</i>-related cancer susceptibility&#x000a0;<sup>2</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AD</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Cancer susceptibility</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_5" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Germline PVs were identified in persons who met relaxed International Cowden Consortium diagnostic criteria &#x00026;/or had elevated Cleveland Clinic scores.&#x000a0;<sup>3</sup></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>PTCH1</i>
<br />
<i>SUFU</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<a href="/sites/books/n/gene/bcns/">Nevoid basal cell carcinoma syndrome (NBCCS)</a>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AD</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Multiple jaw keratocysts &#x00026;/or basal cell carcinomas</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Skeletal anomalies</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Ectopic calcification</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>In many persons, a recognizable appearance w/macrocephaly, frontal bossing, coarse facial features, &#x00026; facial milia</div></li></ul>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_5" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Dermatologic findings &#x00026; developmental features in PHTS &#x00026; NBCCS are quite different.</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>SDHB</i>
<br />
<i>SDHC</i>
<br />
<i>SDHD</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><i>SDHx</i>-related cancer susceptibility&#x000a0;<sup>2</sup> (See also <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/paragangliomas/">Hereditary Paraganglioma-Pheochromocytoma Syndromes</a>.)</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AD</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Cancer susceptibility</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_5" rowspan="2" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Germline PVs were identified in persons who met relaxed International Cowden Consortium diagnostic criteria &#x00026;/or had elevated Cleveland Clinic scores.&#x000a0;<sup>3</sup></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>SEC23B</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><i>SEC23B</i> -related cancer susceptibility <sup>2</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AD</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Thyroid cancer</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>STK11</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><a href="/sites/books/n/gene/pjs/">Peutz-Jeghers syndrome</a> (PJS)</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AD</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>GI polyposis</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Mucocutaneous pigmentation</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Cancer predisposition</div></li></ul>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_5" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">PJS-related polyps are histologically quite different from PHTS hamartomatous polyps. PJS polyps are often symptomatic (intussusception, rectal bleeding); PHTS-related polyps are rarely so.</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>WWP1</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><i>WWP1</i>-related cancer susceptibility&#x000a0;<sup>2</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AD</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Oligopolyposis</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Colorectal cancer</div></li></ul>
</td><td headers="hd_h_phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_gen_1_1_1_5" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Germline PVs were identified in persons who met relaxed International Cowden Consortium diagnostic criteria &#x00026;/or had elevated Cleveland Clinic scores.&#x000a0;<sup>3</sup></td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">AD = <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-dominant/">autosomal dominant</a>; CNS = central nervous system; DVT = deep vein thrombosis; GI = gastrointestinal; MOI = <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/mode-of-inheritance/">mode of inheritance</a>; PHTS = <i>PTEN</i> hamartoma tumor syndrome; PV = <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a></p></div></dd><dt>1. </dt><dd><div id="phts.TF.3.1"><p class="no_margin">Proteus syndrome is not inherited: there are no confirmed occurrences of vertical transmission or sib recurrence.</p></div></dd><dt>2. </dt><dd><div id="phts.TF.3.2"><p class="no_margin">
<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2020.r150">Yehia &#x00026; Eng [2020]</a>
</p></div></dd><dt>3. </dt><dd><div id="phts.TF.3.3"><p class="no_margin">The relaxed operational diagnostic criteria of the International Cowden Consortium (ICC) refers to individuals fulfilling pathognomonic criteria, or at least two criteria; either major or minor.
