class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gonadal-mosaicism/">gonadal mosaicism</a>.</div></li></ul><p><b>Offspring of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a>.</b> Each child of an individual with PHTS has a 50% chance of inheriting the <i>PTEN</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>.</p><p><b>Other family members.</b> The risk to other family members depends on the status of the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a>'s parents: if a parent has the <i>PTEN</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>, the parent's family members may be at risk.</p></div><div id="phts.Related_Genetic_Counseling_Issues"><h3>Related Genetic Counseling Issues</h3><p>See Management, <a href="#phts.Evaluation_of_Relatives_at_Risk">Evaluation of Relatives at Risk</a> for information on evaluating at-risk relatives for the purpose of early diagnosis and treatment.</p><p><b>Predictive testing</b> for at-risk asymptomatic family members requires prior identification of the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> <i>PTEN</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> in the family.</p><p><b>Genetic cancer risk assessment and counseling.</b> For a comprehensive description of the medical, psychosocial, and ethical ramifications of identifying at-risk individuals through cancer risk assessment with or without <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/molecular-genetic-testing/">molecular genetic testing</a>, see <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/about-cancer/causes-prevention/genetics/risk-assessment-pdq" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">Cancer Genetics Risk Assessment and Counseling &#x02013; for health professionals</a> (part of PDQ<sup>&#x000ae;</sup>, National Cancer Institute).</p><p>
<b>Family planning</b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>The optimal time for determination of genetic risk and discussion of the availability of prenatal/<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/preimplantation-genetic-testing/">preimplantation genetic testing</a> is before pregnancy. </div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>It is appropriate to offer <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genetic-counseling/">genetic counseling</a> (including discussion of potential risks to offspring and reproductive options) to young adults who are affected or at risk.</div></li></ul></div><div id="phts.Prenatal_Testing_and_Preimplantatio"><h3>Prenatal Testing and Preimplantation Genetic Testing</h3><p>Once the <i>PTEN</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> has been identified in an affected family member, prenatal and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/preimplantation-genetic-testing/">preimplantation genetic testing</a> are possible.</p><p>Differences in perspective may exist among medical professionals and within families regarding the use of prenatal and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/preimplantation-genetic-testing/">preimplantation genetic testing</a>. While most health care professionals would consider use of prenatal and preimplantation genetic testing to be a personal decision, discussion of these issues may be helpful.</p></div></div><div id="phts.Resources"><h2 id="_phts_Resources_">Resources</h2><p>
<i>The following disease-specific and/or umbrella support organizations and/or registries are listed for the benefit of individuals with this disorder and their families. <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app1/">here</a>.</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div><div id="phts.molgen.TB" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table B.</span></h3><div class="caption"><p>OMIM Entries for PTEN Hamartoma Tumor Syndrome (<a href="/omim/158350,601728,605309" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">View All in OMIM</a>) </p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/table/phts.molgen.TB/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__phts.molgen.TB_lrgtbl__"><table><tbody><tr><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<a href="/omim/158350" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">158350</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">COWDEN SYNDROME 1; CWS1</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<a href="/omim/601728" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">601728</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">PHOSPHATASE AND TENSIN HOMOLOG; PTEN</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<a href="/omim/605309" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">605309</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">MACROCEPHALY/AUTISM SYNDROME</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div id="phts.Molecular_Pathogenesis"><h3>Molecular Pathogenesis</h3><p><i>PTEN</i> encodes phosphatidylinositol 3,4,5-trisphosphate 3-phosphatase and dual-specificity protein phosphatase PTEN, which belongs to a subclass of phosphatases called dual-specificity phosphatases that remove phosphate groups from tyrosine as well as serine and threonine. PTEN localizes to specific nuclear and cytoplasmic components. The <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/wild_type/">wild type</a> protein is a major lipid phosphatase that downregulates the PI3K/AKT pathway to cause G1 cell cycle arrest and apoptosis. In addition, phosphatase appears to play an important role in inhibition of cell migration and spreading, as well as downregulating several cell cyclins. It appears that nuclear PTEN mediates cell cycle arrest, while cytoplasmic PTEN is required for apoptosis [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2018.t121">Yehia &#x00026; Eng 2018</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2019.452">Yehia et al 2019</a>].</p><p>Germline pathogenic variants have been found throughout <i>PTEN</i> and include <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/missense/">missense</a>, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/nonsense-variant/">nonsense</a>, and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/splice-site/">splice site</a> variants, small deletions, insertions, and large deletions. Nearly 40% of pathogenic variants are found in <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exon/">exon</a> 5, which encodes the phosphate core motif [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2019.452">Yehia et al 2019</a>]. Most pathogenic variants are unique, although several recurrent pathogenic variants have been reported (see <a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/table/phts.T.pten_hamartoma_tumor_syndrome_rec_1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobphtsTptenhamartomatumorsyndromerec1">Table 6</a>).</p><p>The majority (76%) of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> pathogenic variants in <i>PTEN</i> predict either truncated PTEN protein, lack of protein (<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/haploinsufficiency/">haploinsufficiency</a>), or dysfunctional protein. Many <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/missense/">missense</a> variants are functionally <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/null/">null</a> and several act as dominant negatives [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.papa.2014.595">Papa et al 2014</a>]. When PTEN is absent, decreased, or dysfunctional, phosphorylation of AKT1 is uninhibited, leading to the inability to activate cell cycle arrest and/or to undergo apoptosis. In addition, through lack of protein phosphatase activity, the mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) pathway is dysregulated, leading to abnormal cell survival [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.yehia.2019.452">Yehia et al 2019</a>].</p><p><b>Mechanism of disease causation</b>. Loss of function</p><p><b><i>PTEN</i>-specific laboratory technical considerations.</b>
<i>PTEN</i> has a highly homologous <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pseudogene/">pseudogene</a> (<i>PTENP1)</i> on <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/chromosome/">chromosome</a> 9 [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.dahia.1998.2403">Dahia et al 1998</a>]. Special consideration should be taken when designing <i>PTEN-</i>specific primers to ensure specificity and to avoid cross-amplification of this pseudogene [<a class="bk_pop" href="#phts.REF.ngeow.2016.63">Ngeow &#x00026; Eng 2016</a>].</p><div id="phts.T.pten_pathogenic_variants_referenc" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 6. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p><i>PTEN</i> Pathogenic Variants Referenced in This <i>GeneReview</i></p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1488/table/phts.T.pten_pathogenic_variants_referenc/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__phts.T.pten_pathogenic_variants_referenc_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_phts.T.pten_pathogenic_variants_referenc_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; border-top: solid 0.50pt; border-right: solid 0.50pt; border-bottom: solid 0.50pt;text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Reference Sequences</th><th id="hd_h_phts.T.pten_pathogenic_variants_referenc_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="border-left: solid 0.50pt; See <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app3/">Quick Reference</a> for an explanation of nomenclature. </p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div></div><div id="phts.Chapter_Notes"><h2 id="_phts_Chapter_Notes_">Chapter Notes</h2><div id="phts.Author_Notes"><h3>Author Notes</h3><p>This chapter is dedicated to the enduring legacy of Charis Eng, MD, PhD, whose pioneering discoveries defined <i>PTEN</i> hamartoma tumor syndrome (PHTS) and transformed its diagnosis and care. Her vision, scientific rigor, and unwavering commitment to patients and trainees continue to guide the field she so profoundly shaped. Dr Eng was the chair and coordinator of the International Cowden Syndrome Consortium, founding Chairwoman of the Cleveland Clinic Genomic Medicine Institute, and a primary researcher in the field of <i>PTEN</i>-related disorders. The Cleveland Clinic Genomic Medicine Institute program was originally the only multidisciplinary Cowden syndrome center in the world, with ongoing clinical and molecular research protocols in PHTS.</p><p>Dr Timothy Tramontana is a medical and biochemical geneticist with experience in rare disorders including inborn errors of metabolism and PHTS. Dr Tramontana oversees the clinical care of PHTS patients in the Cleveland Clinic's <a href="https://my.clevelandclinic.org/departments/genomics/specialties/pten-clinic" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">PTEN Multidisciplinary Clinic and Center of Excellence</a>, founded by the late Charis Eng, MD, PhD. </p><p>Dr Lamis Yehia has long-standing expertise in the genetic basis and clinical <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a> of PHTS. She is a recognized leader in <i>PTEN</i>-related disorders and has played a key role in the discovery of new genetic causes and understanding disease modifiers. Dr Yehia has played leading roles in the translational efforts of the PTEN Center of Excellence at Cleveland Clinic, which houses one of the largest databases and biobanks from this group of patients.</p><p>Dr Yehia (<a href="mailto:dev@null" data-email="gro.fcc@laihey" class="oemail">gro.fcc@laihey</a>) is actively involved in clinical research regarding individuals with PHTS. Dr Tramontana (<a href="mailto:dev@null" data-email="gro.fcc@tnomart" class="oemail">gro.fcc@tnomart</a>) is actively seeing patients with PHTS and would be happy to communicate with persons who have any questions regarding diagnosis of PHTS or other considerations. </p><p>Contact Dr Yehia or <a href="mailto:dev@null" data-email="gro.fcc@netp" class="oemail">gro.fcc@netp</a> to inquire about review of <i>PTEN</i> variants of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/uncertain-significance/">uncertain significance</a>. </p></div><div id="phts.Acknowledgments"><h3>Acknowledgments</h3><p>We are eternally grateful to the many patients and families who have participated in our research and who continue to educate us in the ever-broadening clinical spectrum of PHTS, without which this review and management recommendations could not have been written. Our PHTS research was continuously supported by the American Cancer Society, the Doris Duke Distinguished Clinical Scientist Award, and by the National Cancer Institute. Dr Charis Eng was the Sondra J and Stephen R Hardis Chair of Cancer Genomic Medicine at the Cleveland Clinic. Dr Yehia was an Ambrose Monell Foundation Cancer Genomic Medicine Fellow at the Cleveland Clinic Genomic Medicine Institute.</p></div><div id="phts.Author_History"><h3>Author History</h3><p>Charis Eng, MD, PhD; Cleveland Clinic (2001-2026)<br />Heather Hampel, MS; Ohio State University (2001-2006)<br />Robert Pilarski, MS; Ohio State University (2001-2006)<br />Jennifer L Stein, MS, CGC; Cleveland Clinic (2006-2009)<br />Timothy Tramontana, MD, MS (2026-present)<br />Lamis Yehia, PhD (2021-present)<br />Kevin M Zbuk, MD; Cleveland Clinic (2006-2009)</p></div><div id="phts.Revision_History"><h3>Revision History</h3><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>28 July 2026 (sw) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>11 February 2021 (sw) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>2 June 2016 (sw) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>23 January 2014 (me) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>21 July 2011 (me) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>5 May 2009 (me) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>10 January 2006 (me) Comprehensive update posted live </div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>17 December 2003 (me) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>29 November 2001 (me) Review posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>10 July 2001 (ce) Original submission</div></li></ul></div></div><div id="phts.References"><h2 id="_phts_References_">References</h2><div id="phts.Published_Guidelines__Consensus_Sta"><h3>Published Guidelines / Consensus Statements</h3><ul class="simple-list"><li class="half_rhythm"><div>American Society of Clinical Oncology. <script type="text/javascript">
    (function($){
        $('.skiplink').each(function(i, item){
            var href = $($(item).attr('href'));
            href.attr('tabindex', '-1').addClass('skiptarget'); // ensure the target can receive focus
            $(item).on('click', function(event){
                event.preventDefault();
                $.scrollTo(href, 0, {
                    onAfter: function(){
                        href.focus();
                    }
                });
            });
        });
    })(jQuery);
</script>
